			<div class="after-portlet after-portlet-lang"><span class="wb-langlinks-edit wb-langlinks-link"><a href="https://www.wikidata.org/wiki/Special:EntityPage/Q165568#sitelinks-wikipedia" title="Edit interlanguage links" class="wbc-editpage">Edit links</a></span></div>

					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_numbering" title="Pope John numbering">Pope John numbering</a></div>
<div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Head of the Catholic Church from 1316 to 1334</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" /><div style="display:inline" class="fn">John XXII</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-subheader role"><a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Rome">Bishop of style="display:inline" class="nickname">Jacques Duèze or d'Euse</div><br /><abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1244</span><br /><div style="display:inline" class="birthplace"><a href="/wiki/Cahors" title="Cahors">Cahors</a>, <a href="/wiki/France_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="France in the Middle Ages">Kingdom of France</a></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">4 December 1334<span style="display:none">(1334-12-04)</span> (aged&#160;89–90)<br /><a href="/wiki/Avignon" Provence</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below"><a href="/wiki/Pope_John_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Pope John (disambiguation)">Other popes  named John</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope John XXII</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ioannes PP. XXII</i>; 1244 – 4 December 1334), born <b>Jacques Duèze</b> (or <b>d'Euse</b>), was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> from 7 August 1316 to his death in December 1334.
</p><p>He was the second and longest-reigning <a href="/wiki/Avignon_Papacy" title="Avignon Papacy">Avignon Pope</a>, elected by the <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave" title="Papal conclave">Conclave</a> of <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">Cardinals</a>, which was assembled in <a href="/wiki/Lyon" title="Lyon">Lyon</a> through the work of <a href="/wiki/Louis_X_of_France" title="Louis X of France">King Louis X's</a> brother Philip, the Count of Poitiers. Like his predecessor, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_V" title="Pope Clement V">Clement V</a>, Pope John centralized power and income in the Papacy and lived a princely life in <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a>.
</p><p>John excommunicated the enemies of Edward II of England, while warning Edward of a possible reassessment of the papal grant of Ireland. He opposed the political policies of <a href="/wiki/Louis_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Louis IV, Holy Roman Emperor">Louis IV of Bavaria</a> as <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a>, which prompted Louis to invade Italy and set up an antipope, <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Nicholas_V" title="Antipope Nicholas V">Nicholas V</a>. John opposed the <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscan</a> understanding of the poverty of Christ and his apostles passing multiple papal bulls to enforce his views. This led <a href="/wiki/William_of_Ockham" title="William of Ockham">William of Ockham</a> to write against unlimited papal power. Following a three year process, John canonized <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a> on 18 July 1323.
</p><p>After surviving an assassination attempt from poison and witchcraft, John passed a papal bull against witchcraft and threatened excommunication for anyone learning it. Facing backlash from his beatific vision sermons, John retracted his statement just before his death. John died in Avignon on 4 December 1334.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_election">Early life and election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life and election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Born in 1244, Jacques was the son of Arnaud Duèze,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972161_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972161-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> who was probably a <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr"><a href="/wiki/Cahorsin" class="mw-redirect" title="Cahorsin">Cahorsin</a></i></span> merchant or banker.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972162_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972162-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He studied canon law at <a href="/wiki/University_of_Montpellier" title="University of Montpellier">Montpellier</a> and theology in <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">Paris</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972163_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972163-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jacques taught civil law at Cahors and later <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">canon</a> at <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972164_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972164-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> By 1295, he was counselor to the Bishop <a href="/wiki/Louis_of_Toulouse" title="Louis of Toulouse">Louis of Toulouse</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972164_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972164-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Following Louis' death in 1297, Jacques was installed as the temporary chancellor of the University of Avignon and by 1299 was canon of Puy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972165_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972165-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> On the recommendation of <a href="/wiki/Charles_II_of_Naples" title="Charles II of Naples">Charles II of Naples</a> he was made Bishop of <a href="/wiki/Fr%C3%A9jus" title="Fréjus">Fréjus</a> in 1300.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972165_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972165-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Jacques was still bishop of Fréjus when in 1307, a knight named William d'Arcis and a priest named Bertrand Recordus attempted to kill him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972166_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972166-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Both were imprisoned and later sentenced to death in 1309.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972166_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972166-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1308, Jacques was appointed chancellor of Charles II,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972165_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972165-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and it was due to his influence that the gladiatorial games in Naples were banned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972167_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972167-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> On 18 March 1310, he was made Bishop of Avignon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972168_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972168-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He delivered legal opinions favorable to the suppression of the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Templar" title="Knights Templar">Templars</a>, but he also defended Boniface VIII and the Bull <i>Unam Sanctam</i>. On 23 December 1312, Clement V made him Cardinal-Bishop of <a href="/wiki/Suburbicarian_Diocese_of_Porto_e_Santa_Rufina" class="mw-redirect" title="Suburbicarian Diocese of Porto e Santa Rufina">Porto-Santa Rufina</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972170_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1972170-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The limits of papal power became a source of conflict between reigning pope <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_V" title="Pope Clement V">Clement V</a> and <a href="/wiki/Henry_VII,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Henry VII, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Henry VII</a>, culminating with the sudden death of Pope Clement V in 1314.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The period that followed was an <a href="/wiki/Interregnum" title="Interregnum">interregnum</a> of two years due to disagreements between the <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholicism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal (Catholicism)">cardinals</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurnham200840_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurnham200840-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> who were split into three factions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELevillain2002848_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELevillain2002848-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> After two years of inaction, <a href="/wiki/Philip_V_of_France" title="Philip V of France">Philip, Count of Poitiers</a> summoned a <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1314%E2%80%931316" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1314–1316">papal conclave</a> of twenty-three cardinals to Lyon in August 1316.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMenache1998280_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMenache1998280-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Philip announced that none of the cardinals would be allowed to leave until they had chosen a new Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMenache1998280_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMenache1998280-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> This conclave elected Jacques on 7 August 1316, who took the name John XXII and was crowned in Lyon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERollo-Koster201545_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERollo-Koster201545-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He set up his residence in Avignon rather than Rome, continuing the <a href="/wiki/Avignon_Papacy" title="Avignon Papacy">Avignon Papacy</a> of his predecessor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPluger2005144_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPluger2005144-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Papacy">Papacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Papacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Stato_della_chiesa,_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII,_1316-1334.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Stato_della_chiesa%2C_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII%2C_1316-1334.JPG/220px-Stato_della_chiesa%2C_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII%2C_1316-1334.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="218" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Stato_della_chiesa%2C_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII%2C_1316-1334.JPG/330px-Stato_della_chiesa%2C_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII%2C_1316-1334.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Stato_della_chiesa%2C_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII%2C_1316-1334.JPG/440px-Stato_della_chiesa%2C_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII%2C_1316-1334.JPG 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="633" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Stato_della_chiesa,_carlino_di_giovanni_XXII,_1316-1334.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Coin" title="Coin">Coin</a> of John XXII</div></div></div>
<p>Upon his election, John XXII gave 35,000 florins, of the 70,000 left to him by Clement V, to the cardinals that had voted for him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeakland1968285-286_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeakland1968285-286-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>He was an excellent administrator and efficient at reorganizing the Catholic Church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters2001118_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters2001118-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He favored a regulation-heavy form of governance.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John XXII was known to have a temper.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John involved himself in the politics and religious movements of many European countries in order to advance the interests of the church. He would routinely write to non-Catholic leaders asserting his authority over them.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His close links with the French crown created widespread distrust of the papacy.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On 7 April 1317, John canonized Louis of Toulouse with the bull Sol oriens.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrunner2011231_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrunner2011231-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECusatoGeltner2009139_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECusatoGeltner2009139-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1318, he sent a letter of thanks to the Muslim ruler <a href="/wiki/Uzbeg_Khan" class="mw-redirect" title="Uzbeg Khan">Uzbeg Khan</a>, who was very tolerant of Christians and treated Christians kindly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEpstein2007133_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEpstein2007133-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John has traditionally been credited with having composed the prayer "<a href="/wiki/Anima_Christi" title="Anima Christi">Anima Christi</a>", which has become the English "Soul of Christ, sanctify me&#160;...".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERubin1991157_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERubin1991157-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 27 March 1329, John condemned many writings of <a href="/wiki/Meister_Eckhart" title="Meister Eckhart">Meister Eckhart</a> as heretical in his papal bull <i>In Agro Dominico</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEckhart198177_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEckhart198177-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He allowed the consuls of Cahors to establish a small university in that city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVerger200667_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVerger200667-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John died in Avignon on 4 December 1334 (aged 89/90).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichardson2009xxi_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichardson2009xxi-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> He was buried, in Avignon, at the cathedral of Notre-Dame-des-Doms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2000238_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2000238-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Edward_II_and_Irish_bishoprics">Edward II and Irish bishoprics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Edward II and Irish bishoprics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John received an embassy, in December 1316, from Edward II of England concerning the empty bishoprics of Dublin and Cashel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt19562_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt19562-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Included in his preferred appointees, Edward was adamant that John not promote any native Irishman to bishop or archbishop.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt19562_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt19562-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> John, promoted, William FitzJohn, bishop of Ossory to the bishopric of Cashel, while Alexander Bicknor, a personal recommendation of Edward, was given Dublin.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt19562-3_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt19562-3-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 1318, John sent a congratulatory letter to Edward on his victory over Edward Bruce.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt19563_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt19563-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He had passed an excommunication of all enemies of Edward and later singled out Robert and Edward Bruce.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt19563_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt19563-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> John had allowed Edward to keep the crusade tax imposed by the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Vienne" title="Council of Vienne">Council of Vienne</a>, while reminding him that England was a papal fief and that he owed John an oath of loyalty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt19563-4_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt19563-4-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> A few months later, John heard from Irish princes petitioning for the replacement of Edward as sovereign lord of Ireland, stating that successive kings of England had not respected the papal grant of Ireland to Henry II of England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrame199879_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrame199879-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> John's letter to Edward inferred that a second papal evaluation of the lordship of Ireland was not out of the question.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatt19564_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatt19564-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization_of_Thomas_Aquinas">Canonization of Thomas Aquinas</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Canonization of Thomas Aquinas">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>By November 1317, Guillelmo de Tocco, Prior of the Benevento Dominican monastery, was heading a preliminary investigation into Thomas Aquinas' sainthood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196736_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196736-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In the Summer of 1318, John  met with de Tocco, who presented letters from the regal heads of Sicily imploring for a papal inquiry into the sainthood of Aquinas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196736_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196736-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> De Tocco also presented John with notarized records of Aquinas' miracles and requests from princes and universities asking for Aquinas' canonization.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196736_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196736-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John chose three cardinals, none of whom was a Dominican, to scrutinize the presented records.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Finding no errors, they gave their recommendation to John.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> John then issued two letters nominating: Umberto, Archbishop of Naples, Angelo, Bishop of Viterbo, and Pandulpho de Sabbello, who was unable to attend, as papal commissioners.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737_33-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>De Tocco gather two of the three papal commissioners in the Archbishop's palace in Naples.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737_33-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The inquiry lasted from 21 July to 18 September 1319.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737_33-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196737-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> On the final day, the documentation of the inquiry was signed, sealed and sent to the Papal Curia at Avignon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Upon receiving the documents John turned them over to a cardinal committee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> On 23 June 1321, John created a new commission consisting of Peter Ferri, Bishop of Agnani, Andrew, Bishop of Terracina, and Pandulpho de Sabbello.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739_34-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> This inquiry lasted fifteen days, during which the commission heard the testimony of one hundred eleven witnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739_34-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196739-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The commission sent finalized documents to the Papal Curia and John XXII canonized Thomas Aquinas formally on 18 July 1323.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196740-41_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerulaitis196740-41-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflict_with_Louis_IV">Conflict with Louis IV</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Conflict with Louis IV">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Prior to John XXII's election, a contest had begun for the Holy Roman Empire's crown between Louis IV of Bavaria and <a href="/wiki/Frederick_I_of_Austria_(Habsburg)" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick I of Austria (Habsburg)">Frederick I of Austria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson201669_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson201669-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Initially, John was neutral and refrained from being an arbiter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson201669_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson201669-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> On 25 November 1314, Louis, with the votes from five of the six electors, was crowned king of the Germans at Aix-la-Chapelle, by the archbishop of Mainz, while Frederick only received two votes the day before.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200434_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200434-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In response, in April 1317, John imposed a new concept, <i>vacante imperio</i>, granting the power to administer the empire to the pope,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilks2008255_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilks2008255-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> which was contained within the papal bull, <i>Si fratrum</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScott201657_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott201657-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ludovico_il_Bavaro.jpeg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Ludovico_il_Bavaro.jpeg/220px-Ludovico_il_Bavaro.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="317" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Ludovico_il_Bavaro.jpeg/330px-Ludovico_il_Bavaro.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/54/Ludovico_il_Bavaro.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="395" data-file-height="569" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ludovico_il_Bavaro.jpeg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Emperor Louis IV</div></div></div>
<p>By 1322, faced with a growing Ghibelline movement in northern Italy, John had instructed his papal legate, Bertrand du Poujet, to call for a crusade against Milan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeattie200726_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeattie200726-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Louis, acting with Imperial powers, sent an army to reinforce Milan in early 1323.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPartner2022316_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPartner2022316-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In response, John detailed violations committed by Louis and upon receiving no response excommunicated him on 23 March 1324 and then deposed him on 11 July.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200435_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200435-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Louis called a council of Italian <a href="/wiki/Guelphs_and_Ghibellines" title="Guelphs and Ghibellines">Ghibellines</a> to Trent, there Louis declared his support for the Spiritualist Franciscans and denounced John as an illegitimate and heretical pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurr2001276_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurr2001276-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200435_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200435-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> John retaliated by declaring any city to offer Louis welcome would be placed under interdict.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200435_42-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200435-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> He received an embassy from Rome which issued an ultimatum of either returning to Rome or suffer the Holy City's submission to Louis IV.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200435_42-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200435-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> John sent his papal legate, Giovanni Orsini, to Rome, but was denied entry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200435_42-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200435-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> In reprisal, Orsini placed Rome under interdict.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200435_42-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200435-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis entered Rome on 17 January 1328.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200436_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200436-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In St. Peter's cathedral, he was consecrated by two sympathetic cardinal bishops, and crowned by Rome's representative, Sciarra della Colonna.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECassell200436_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECassell200436-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Pietro Rainalducci<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> was created <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Nicholas_V" title="Antipope Nicholas V">Antipope Nicholas V</a> in May 1328, by Louis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliams199844_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams199844-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Following Louis' coronation of Nicholas V, Franciscan Minister General Michael Cesena, Bonagrazia Bergamo, and William of Ockham all fled Avignon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOcker2022120_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOcker2022120-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> In 1330, Antipope Nicholas V submitted just before the Pope's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuval-Arnould20021040_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuval-Arnould20021040-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between November 1331 and March 1332, John made four questionable sermons concerning the <a href="/wiki/Beatific_vision" title="Beatific vision">beatific vision</a>. Louis, sensing an opportunity to remove him, conspired with cardinal Napoleone Orsini to depose John at an ecumenical council.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2000238_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2000238-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> John, however, became ill and reversed his statements before dying.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2000238_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBrien2000238-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Franciscan_poverty">Franciscan poverty</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Franciscan poverty">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Absolute_poverty_of_Christ" title="Absolute poverty of Christ">absolute poverty of Christ</a> and <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_poverty" title="Apostolic poverty">apostolic poverty</a></div>
<p>John XXII was determined to suppress what he considered to be the excesses of the Spirituals, who contended eagerly for the view that Christ and his apostles had possessed absolutely nothing, citing <a href="/wiki/Pope_Nicholas_III" title="Pope Nicholas III">Pope Nicholas III</a>'s bull <i>Exiit qui seminat</i> in support of their view.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurnham200873_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurnham200873-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> On 7 October 1317,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrieco2013149_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrieco2013149-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> John XXII promulgated the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> <i>Quorumdam exigit</i>, clarifying the previous bulls issued by Nicholas III and Clement V, <i>Exiit qui seminat</i> and <i>Exivi de paradiso</i>, and stating unequivocally that obedience was greater than chastity or poverty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurnham200846_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurnham200846-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> On 26 March 1322, with <i>Quia nonnunquam</i>, he removed the ban on discussion of <i>Exiit qui seminat</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcClure201729_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcClure201729-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> and commissioned experts to examine the idea of poverty based on belief that Christ and the apostles owned nothing. The experts disagreed among themselves, but the majority condemned the idea on the grounds that it would condemn the church's right to have possessions. The Franciscan chapter held in <a href="/wiki/Perugia" title="Perugia">Perugia</a> in June 1322 responded with two encyclicals stating that all judgments made by the Roman Church were to be regarded as final and could not be revoked if they were not erroneous.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202275_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202275-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> By the bull <i>Ad conditorem canonum</i> of 8 December 1322,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202275_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202275-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> John XXII declared it ridiculous to pretend that every scrap of food given to the friars and eaten by them belonged to the pope, refused to accept ownership over the goods of the Franciscans in future and granted them exemption from the rule that absolutely forbade ownership of anything even in common, thus forcing them to accept ownership. On 12 November 1323, he issued the bull <i>Quum inter nonnullos</i>, which declared "erroneous and heretical" the doctrine that Christ and his apostles had no possessions whatsoever.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchatz1996117-118_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchatz1996117-118-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETierney1972181_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETierney1972181-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg/220px-Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg/330px-Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg/440px-Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="3888" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Tombeau_de_Jean_XXII.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Tomb of John XXII in the Treasury room of the <a href="/wiki/Cath%C3%A9drale_Notre-Dame_des_Doms_d%27Avignon" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathédrale Notre-Dame des Doms d&#39;Avignon">Cathédrale Notre-Dame des Doms d'Avignon</a></div></div></div>
<p>Influential members of the order protested, such as the minister general <a href="/wiki/Michael_of_Cesena" title="Michael of Cesena">Michael of Cesena</a>, the English provincial <a href="/wiki/William_of_Ockham" title="William of Ockham">William of Ockham</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bonagratia_of_Bergamo" title="Bonagratia of Bergamo">Bonagratia of Bergamo</a>. In reply to the argument of his opponents that Nicholas III's bull <i>Exiit qui seminat</i> was fixed and irrevocable, John XXII issued the bull <i>Quia quorundam</i> on 10 November 1324,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202275_55-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202275-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> in which he declared that it cannot be inferred from the words of the 1279 bull that Christ and the apostles had nothing, adding: "Indeed, it can be inferred rather that the Gospel life lived by Christ and the Apostles did not exclude some possessions in common, since living 'without property' does not require that those living thus should have nothing in common."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202277_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDi_Cristo202277-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> With the bull <i>Quia vir reprobus</i> of 16 November 1329, John replied to Michael of Cesena's <i>Appellatio</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett200352_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett200352-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Beatific_vision_controversy">Beatific vision controversy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Beatific vision controversy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John XXII argued that those who died in the faith did not see the presence of God until the <a href="/wiki/Last_Judgment" title="Last Judgment">Last Judgment</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJuhász2015117-118_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuhász2015117-118-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> This caused a theological controversy concerning the <a href="/wiki/Beatific_vision" title="Beatific vision">beatific vision</a>. He continued this argument for a time in sermons while he was pope, although he never taught it in official documents. In January 1334, in complete opposition to John XXII, the Faculty of Theology of Sorbonne stated that after death the blessed souls were raised to the beatific vision after death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJuhász2015118_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuhász2015118-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> He eventually backed down from his position the day before his death, and agreed that those who died in grace do indeed immediately enjoy the beatific vision.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJuhász2015119_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuhász2015119-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Role_in_witchcraft_suppression">Role in witchcraft suppression</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Role in witchcraft suppression">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The thought of witchcraft seemed to be in its early stages, while Kors states John XXII had a personal reason for setting out to stop witchcraft.  Kors points to the fact that Pope John had been the victim of an assassination attempt via poisoning and sorcery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters2001118_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters2001118-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> As such, Pope John's involvement with witchcraft persecution can be officially traced to his 1326 papal bull <i>Super illius specula</i> in which he laid out a description of those who engage in witchcraft.  Pope John also warned people against not only learning magic or teaching it but against the more “execrable” act of performing magic.  Pope John stated that anyone who did not heed his “most charitable” warning would be excommunicated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters200182_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters200182-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Pope John officially declared witchcraft to be heresy, and thus it could be tried under the Inquisition.  Although this was the official ruling for the church, Pope John's first order dealing with magic being tried by the Inquisition was in a letter written in 1320 by Cardinal William of Santa Sabina.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters2001118_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorsPeters2001118-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>  The letter was addressed to the Inquisitors of Carcassonne and Toulouse.  In the letter Cardinal William states that with the authority of Pope John the Inquisitors there were to investigate witches by “whatever means available” as if witches were any other heretic. The letter went on to describe the actions of those who would be seen as witches and extended power to the Inquisition for the prosecution of any and all cases that fit any part of the description laid out in the letter.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_XXII&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Bernard_Jarre" title="Bernard Jarre">Bernard Jarre</a> – Catholic cardinal</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Spondent_Pariter" class="mw-redirect" title="Spondent Pariter">Spondent Pariter</a></i> – papal <a href="/wiki/Decretal" title="Decretal">decretal</a> issued by Pope John XXII forbidding fraudulent <a href="/wiki/Alchemy" title="Alchemy">alchemy</a>.</li></ul>
